#10428. Google AI Ultra upgrade adds YouTube Premium Family

This perk is available for both the Google AI Ultra 5x plan at $99.99/month and AI Ultra 20x at $199.99/month.

For $200 a month Google should remove all ads from all its services and disable tracking. Not only are they making money off of you but they are also charging $200 a month for AI that trained on your data. Nasty business.

YouTube Premium Family costs $26.99⁠/month and lets you add up to five family members in your household. It offers ad-free YouTube and YouTube Music with background playback and offline downloads, as well as other benefits like Ask Music.

AI Pro at $19.99/month only offers Premium Lite, which excludes YouTube Music benefits.

Google says this has been a highly requested feature. It makes the upgrade from Pro to Ultra a more compelling offering if you need the storage or are a big Gemini user with several family members. AI Pro with 10 TB is $49.99/month, with AI Ultra 5x offering 20 TB and AI Ultra 20x boosting that to 30 TB.

#10427. Tech lawsuits defined 2026 — and lawmakers want more

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With just a few weeks remaining, lawmakers are trying to pass new protections for victims of online harm before the end of 2026, targeting deepfake creators and online platforms that block users from filing lawsuits.

On Tuesday, members of a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hosted a hearing calling for the end of forced arbitration for online harms. Their immediate target was Roblox, which they accused of evading legal complaints about children allegedly groomed on the platform by enforcing its own forced arbitration clause against users who agreed to the terms as minors, in some cases even under the age of 10. Separately, a few blocks away, Reps. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-NY) and Laurel Lee (R-FL) brought hotel heiress Paris Hilton to Capitol Hill to advocate for the Defiance Act, which would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors,” Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO), who chairs the Judiciary subcommittee, told reporters after the Roblox hearing. “The more I see these companies evade regulations, the more I think there is nothing as powerful as a jury.” Hawley introduced a new bill with ranking member Dick Durbin (D-IL) to render arbitration agreements unenforceable for disputes over alleged child sexual exploitation or “child cyber injury,” which could include “reputational harm, mental suffering,” or death.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors”

Roblox has sought to enforce its arbitration clause in lawsuits it’s facing over allegations that users were groomed on the gaming platform as minors, despite the company’s claims of protections. Roblox is facing about 180 lawsuits in a consolidated case over its alleged role in failing to protect children. Under the arbitration process, the public would not get to see legal discovery like the kind that’s come to light under recent cases against Meta. Hawley called the jury system “corruption-proof” and “an incredibly big hammer.”

Roblox has denied that it’s trying to dodge accountability. “Safety has been central to Roblox since we launched. We require age checks for chat, place users under 16 in age-based accounts with protective defaults, and block images and video in chat,” Roblox’s global head of public policy Nicky Jackson Colaço said in a statement. “A legal position about arbitration is not a measure of a company’s safety record, and a hearing built around it fails to advance safety.”

Congress has only a little more than three more weeks left in session before the end of the year, and a new Democratic majority will be sworn in come January. Republicans are expected to do their best to push through President Donald Trump’s nominees for judgeships and executive branch posts like FCC commissioner in the lame-duck session, as well as try to pass year-end bills like the National Defense Authorization Act, which often includes a range of other policy items. But some lawmakers are holding out hope that they can open up courthouses to new litigation over deepfake abuse and child predation.

“You will be amazed by how quickly the company will change its behavior once it is sued in public court”

The Defiance Act would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators for damages, in an attempt to raise the cost of creating these kinds of images. Hilton visited Washington earlier this year to share how an intimate non-deepfake video of her at age 19 shared without her consent impacted her life, and she used her story to advocate for the Defiance Act. At Tuesday’s press conference, she said she would come back to Congress as many times as necessary for the bill to become law. “Some people might have thought that was the end of my fight,” Hilton said. “But when I make a promise to survivors, I keep it.”

Hilton said over 50 million people have been victimized by sexually explicit deepfakes. “For me personally, strangers have created over 100,000 AI-generated explicit images and videos portraying me in the most graphic and disturbing ways imaginable,” Hilton said. “If this can happen to someone with my resources and my platform, imagine how powerless a young girl might feel facing this completely alone.”

Hilton’s comments were followed by Elliston Berry, an advocate who said a classmate made an explicit deepfake image of her friend group “from an innocent social media post” when she was 14, and distributed it throughout her school. “In a matter of seconds, my sense of security disappeared,” she said. “For me, the Defiance Act means recognition that victims deserve to be heard, that their experience matters, and that their right to seek justice should not depend on how quickly technology advances.”

#10425. Jensen Huang question gets reporter ejected from Trump-Xi-Putin summit

It seems that controversial questions are frowned upon.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A Bloomberg reporter was ejected from a historic trilateral meeting between the leaders of the United States, China, and Russia after reportedly asking a sensitive question to the U.S. President while in the presence of the leader of the Chinese Communist Party. According to The Washington Post, Bloomberg News journalist Annmarie Hordern, the chief political correspondent and co-host of the media outlet’s leading program, Bloomberg Surveillance, asked, “President Trump, has Jensen Huang been disloyal to the United States?”

A second reporter had then started asking a second question before Chinese officials shushed the group. It’s unclear if Trump was able to answer both these questions. Senior White House Correspondent for Fox News Jacqui Heinrich said on X that China, the host country of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit where the trilateral meeting was happening along the side, told journalists that “yelling questions is considered extremely rude” and that anyone doing so “will be pulled from the room,” possibly implying the nature of the questions' delivery, rather than its content, was behind the decision.

Jensen Huang’s sudden resignation as the CEO of Nvidia has been a major scandal in the past few weeks. Even though he admitted to having an affair, he did not name the third party, reported to be Alice Huang, a Chinese executive for Megaspeed who’s also accused of diverting $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia chips into China and passing along information to Chinese intelligence.

The U.S. and China have been locked in a rivalry in recent years, with artificial intelligence being one of the industries that the Chinese Communist Party is focusing on. This race to achieve “supremacy” in AI has led the White House to impose export controls on its most powerful chips and to bar hardware containing U.S. tech from making its way into China. But even though Trump made a 180-degree turn and finally allowed Nvidia H100 chips into China in December 2025, provided the company can secure export licenses, the most advanced Blackwell and upcoming Vera Rubin chips from Nvidia are still out of reach for Chinese companies. Aside from that, Washington is also closing a loophole that allowed Chinese AI firms to take advantage of American compute by exporting their data to data centers located in other countries. Despite all those controls, rumors of AI chip smuggling still persist, with several individuals, including Supermicro co-founder Yih-Shyan “Wally” Liaw, getting arrested for chip diversion.

China also puts tighter control on its journalists and censors sensitive topics. As the host of the APEC summit and the trilateral meeting, it could exclude reporters that it thinks violate its standards. Heinrich said that the White House advocated for Hordern’s inclusion in the trilateral meeting, but it seems that the Chinese refused to budge on their decision.

#10424. New gradient icons roll out to Google Messages & Contacts

Google this week completed the rollout of its new gradient icons with updates for Messages and Contacts.

The icon shapes remain the same for all three communication apps, though they are slightly larger and better fill the circular backgrounds. Contacts is still a head and body, while Messages remains overlapping chat bubbles and Phone a handset. This redesign is not particularly disruptive.

A lighter shade of blue is leveraged, while all feature a gradient effect. This results in the logos feeling less heavy and a bit more modern.

Google describes the new Messages icon as “sharper, crisper, and clearer than ever before.” The SMS/RCS client did not roll out any other changes with the logo redesign this week. The big homepage redesign is still in the beta channel.

Google Contacts was the last Android icon to be refreshed, with version 4.92.26.x rolling out on Wednesday evening. The Phone by Google update rolled out on Monday to stable users.

It took two weeks from announcement to full availability across the three apps.

#10423. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

#10422. The leaked Lenovo Legion Go Beam could be the affordable handheld we need

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For a while there, it seemed like Lenovo had given up on selling handheld gaming PCs, hiking the price of its Legion Go 2 and Legion Go S far higher than other RAM-starved competitors. A decent one can easily cost $1,000 if not $2,000 now. But it looks like Lenovo might be about to announce a relatively cheap handheld once again— the Legion Go Beam.

Windows Latest has leaked images of the handheld, and there’s no question it’s designed to be a value alternative to even the company’s least expensive Legion Go S variant. While it’s got the same big stippled grips and triggers for comfort, there’s only one USB-C port on the top instead of two, a comparatively cheap-looking D-pad, no touchpad, and only a single tiny intake and exhaust for its fan.

There’s no SteamOS logo where that would go, so it likely runs Windows instead… but then there’s that name, “Beam.” Perhaps you’ll “beam” your games to it because it’s a streaming and cloud gaming handheld, instead of one that primarily plays games on its own chip? Maybe that’s why it doesn’t need as much airflow as previous handhelds?

Perhaps there’s a partnership with Xbox Cloud Gaming specifically, and that’s why it’s all lit up in Xbox green?

But it might just be green because it looks nice with white, which has been the “value” color for handhelds so far (see: ROG Ally, Xbox Ally, and the Windows-based Legion Go S). And it might be “Beam” because there’s a literal beam of light on the bottom, even though the first thing I do with most handhelds is turn any and all distracting RGB lighting off. Or maybe it’s “Beam” just because it sounds like a friendly, fun, value-oriented name, instead of something “S” or “Pro.”

I won’t begin to speculate on the Legion Go Beam’s price, except to say that “cheap” may be relative. The Legion Go S was originally supposed to cost $499, but wound up costing far more except on the occasional sale. The Legion Go 2 was supposed to start at $1,099 but wound up costing $1,500 and up after RAMageddon. Streaming-first handhelds have been far cheaper, though, like the $249 Sony PlayStation Portal.

Lenovo has a history of announcing its new gaming devices at CES in January each year, including previous Legion Go handhelds, so I’m expecting to see an official announcement of the Lenovo Legion Go Beam then. Even if so, that doesn’t mean it’ll be available in January — there’s often a many-month lag.

#10420. Calling in sick: Vice City

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Not sure if you’ve heard, but the biggest video game of all time is out today: buying Anthropic stock. Yes, in case you haven’t already blown all of your money on the GTA VI Ultimate Edition, you can try your luck at buying one of the most anticipated IPOs of all time when ANT goes public on the Nasdaq later today.

But you’re likely up, playing the game that is, according to Metacritic, already among the best of all time. It’s currently sitting at a near-perfect 99/100 on the aggregator, with reviewers calling it “a masterpiece” and “gaming’s… greatest love story.” It’s a game in which you can do basically anything you want, from the violent to the vacuous.

More than anything, though, I’m just excited to discover all the different ways the game allows you to find and consume coffee.

As I’m sure happened in every newsroom, the conversation this week turned to whether traffic will meaningfully decline today as up to 1.5 million people “call in sick” to play GTA VI. If you’ve been awake since the crack of dawn trying to meander your way to Vice City, you’re just one of many folks experiencing the rarest of moments in 2026: a singular, unifying cultural event.

My biggest question isn’t whether gamers will play GTA VI but whether normies will. Previous games in the series, particularly GTA V, sold hundreds of millions of copies and still draw countless players today. GTA VI feels even more inviting to those who just want to spend time in its sandbox to do some fishing, dancing, or flying. One reviewer even called it a “cozy game” where he just spent time walking around a zoo.

While the size, scale, and impact of GTA VI are exponentially higher, the last time I heard people talking about a game in this way was during the pandemic, when everyone was hunkering down for a winter of Animal Crossing.

By the way, GTA VI has a functioning smartphone that can be used for making calls, sending texts, taking photos, looking at “Instagram,” and setting reminders, among other things. It’s yet another minor detail in a game full of them. Plus, it looks like an original Pixel.

Usually with sponsored content, I have to spend the whole time convincing you that the product is worth buying. But RedPocket’s Black Friday deals do all that work on their own.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the Black Friday sale, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Explore RedPocket’s plans or compare the annual offers. This is one of the best Black Friday decisions you’re going to make this month, and maybe ever.

#10419. Grand Theft Auto VI understands just how important a smartphone is

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI, in many ways, tries to simulate real life. And life in 2026 would not be complete without a smartphone. GTA VI understands this and makes the phone not just a central part of the experience, but an object that exists within the world. You may try to escape into a fantasy of robbing banks and fighting drug lords, but you can’t get away from texts from your mom or nagging fitness app notifications.

Seeing a smartphone in a game isn’t a new phenomenon. The device has become a natural stand-in for the main menu in many games, giving players an interface that feels intuitive, because a phone is something they use every day. But the phone in GTA VI is different for a few reasons. One is that it behaves much like any other object in the game, whether it’s a gun or a bottle of beer. The action doesn’t pause when you use your phone. Instead, the game switches to a first-person viewpoint, where you can still walk around — or drive, if you’re feeling ambitious — while reading texts or flipping through a fictional version of Instagram.

Rob Nelson, the co-studio head at developer Rockstar North and producer on GTA VI, says that it would’ve felt “archaic” to have a static phone that worked like those in other games, or the one in GTA V, which appeared as a pop-up at the bottom of the screen across from the minimap. “We felt that it was important to make it physical so you have to look at it and put your attention on it, including while driving,” he explains. “It’s about finding the line between realism and player convenience. But the act of being lost in your phone felt like something worth doing, even though it could mean slightly more inconvenience. In this game you are committing to using it.” (Of course, texting and driving, like much of what you do in GTA VI, is both very dangerous and very illegal.)

You have to interact with the phone in the game constantly. Often the story plays out through phone calls and text messages, and you have a surprisingly large array of apps to use that serve a real purpose in the game. You manage your money in a banking app, use a rideshare app when you need a lift, track your workouts in a fitness app, scroll through a social network complete with comments and the ability to like posts, and sideload an AI tool that scans nearby cars so you can see how easy they’ll be to steal. You can even change the wallpaper and ringtone. (GTA VI has two main characters that you swap between, and each has their own phone, though they’re functionally identical.)

What’s most impressive is how all of these apps interact with each other. For instance, after pulling off a major heist, a friend might text you a news story about it, asking “was this you!?” You can then click the link, open up the news app, and read an actual article about what went down. These kinds of interactions can be functional, like when another character texts you a location, which you can then add to your map so the GPS can guide you there. Other times, they add texture to the world or depth to the characters. GTA VI is a love story about two criminals named Jason and Lucia, and you play as both. At one point, while controlling Lucia, I got a text asking for a selfie. I opened up the camera, shot a pic of Lucia drinking a latte, and sent it over. Jason replied immediately — and their relationship got a boost.

Phones are so fully integrated into the game that they can even interrupt the characters’ sleep. At the end of each day, you can click on a bed to rest for the night. But neither Jason nor Lucia will instantly fall asleep when you do — instead, they’ll sit down and pull out their phone, and you have the option to doomscroll or catch up on texts instead of going to sleep right away. Nelson says that this was implemented because the developers wanted to include the ability for the characters to sit on the bed at night, much like a real couple in a shared living space might do. “We needed something to do aside from just sitting there staring at nothing,” he says.

If nothing else, GTA VI’s phone shows how alike we all are: Even bank robbers being hunted by the police can’t seem to stop scrolling. “Phones are a constant part of our lives,” Nelson says of the evolution, “even more than they were in 2013.”

#10417. Thousands of vulnerable fridges patched against cyberattacks in secret operation

Hacks across Europe caused supermarket fridges to reach temperatures of 50 degrees Celsius, but a repeat of the issue isn't expected in the U.K.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following devastating hacks that caused European fridges to reach 50 degrees Celsius, destroying food stocks, the UK has implemented nationwide patching to ensure similar attacks won't affect its fridges. While it was thought that the UK had avoided similar hacks because it used different devices from those used in mainland Europe, an even worse issue was just waiting to be discovered. The BBC reports that a different flaw was discovered by security researchers in late October, one that could have caused even worse damage if it had been identified by hackers. Since then, work has been carried out to harden security across the U.K., with fridges in supermarkets, warehouses, and elsewhere now protected.

Refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions were the first to identify the issue before the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) all became involved in working on the problem. As a precaution, thousands of devices that control tens of thousands of fridges were blocked from accessing the internet, with supermarket IT workers scrambling to sometimes physically isolate devices by removing network cables.

"From a business perspective, it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," one supermarket worker warned.

The issue that they were trying to address rested within the most popular refrigeration controllers used across the U.K., made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM). It was found that these controllers had a similar authentication security flaw to those hacked across Europe, although RDM points out that hacking its devices would have required a specific piece of hardware. However, that hardware is freely available.

RDM has since confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on any of its controllers throughout the patching process.

The situation in France, Belgium, and other European countries was much more dire. After initially hitting France in October, a further wave of attacks swept through supermarkets and even blood banks. Even the fridges at a U.S. military base were not safe. While no responsibility for the attacks has been taken, France has so far laid the blame at the door of Iran — something the country's government denies.

#10415. The wait begins for GTA VI on PC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI is finally here for millions of players who own a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X / S, and those who plan to buy a console, despite rising costs this year, just to play it. As you have likely already noticed, the launch is inescapable, and that’s especially tough if you’re a PC gamer.

I’m sorry to remind you that the clock has only just now started ticking down to the unannounced yet inevitable release of the PC version. Good luck with avoiding Twitch and browsing the internet while trying to remain oblivious to the violent (and romantic) delights that await you.

Seeing Leonida in true 4K at an unlocked frame rate with full ray-tracing effects and extended draw distances, among other graphical improvements, might be a thing you can do on your PC as early as sometime in Q1 2027, if recent reports are accurate. Regardless, early 2027 is not right now. Speaking as someone with a capable gaming desktop, I’m sad to say that FOMO has already gotten the best of me. I bought GTA VI on the PS5 and will probably double-dip and buy it again on PC, just like I did with GTA V and Red Dead Redemption 2. The psychology of me, a weak-willed consumer, is one that publisher Rockstar Games and really any other company in its position would be happy to exploit.

The PC version of GTA VI will launch, eventually, in a very different landscape than the PC ports of Red Dead Redemption 2 and GTA V did. Despite the recent downturn in PC sales driven by increased RAM prices, there are considerably more players on PC now than there were a decade ago. SteamDB’s charts show that Steam’s concurrent users alone have more than doubled (from around 20 million to over 40 million now) since 2020.

A chunk of those people are likely made up of console expats who decided to migrate to PC for various reasons. Some wanted to experience the fun of building their own desktop, while some were mainly convinced by the fact that you can get more power for your money with a PC than with a PlayStation 5 Pro or Xbox Series X. For a subset of people, the convenience of getting something like a Steam Deck to bring your PC game library with you anywhere more than makes up for its lack of power. There are many other justifiable reasons for making PC your main gaming platform, like getting faster access to discounts on digital games, not paying for online multiplayer, ultrawide monitor resolutions, being able to buy games from one of several platforms, and having one device that you can use for work and play.

Rockstar seems poised to deliver the definitive version of GTA VI on PC on its own terms, and I’m interested to see what the end result will be. In addition to building the best-looking version of the game made to run well across countless configurations, its PC optimization efforts are likely also more focused than before on ensuring decent performance on lower-end hardware (including PC handhelds). With a 200-plus-gigabyte install size on consoles, I dread how big it’ll be on PC.

Ideally, the PC version will launch alongside an improved version of GTA Online (whether it’s free to play is anyone’s guess), and maybe with upgraded GTA roleplay tech to support it. I’m hopeful that the PC version of GTA VI will come in early 2027, but if the numerous delays of the console versions were any indication, the developer will take all the time it needs to get it right.

Update, 9:55AM ET, November 19th: The SteamDB data cited now specifies that it’s concurrent users, not the number of users total.

#10413. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

#10409. Phison details SSD with a built-in NPU and 64GB of memory that supports AI frameworks on the drive

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Neither design has a product name, a named controller or a sample date yet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Phison detailed two new enterprise SSD designs at Supercomputer 2026 (SC26) that aim to answer two separate problems: one with a built-in NPU and 64GB of 130 GB/s memory that supports running PyTorch, vLLM, OpenVINO, and llama.cpp directly on the drive, and another that merges fast pSLC cache with large QLC capacity behind just one controller. The company notes that “many of the products and features mentioned are still in development” and will arrive once finalized.

The computational-storage design’s premise is that a server can offload more to the SSD, which runs AI code on the data where it already resides and sends back results, instead of whole datasets. The pSLC-QLC design, meanwhile, puts the cache inside each drive, which may reduce system complexity, as every drive bay contributes usable capacity and none goes to dedicated cache drives.

Phison says that the integrated NPU is expected to deliver “36,800 DMIPS of compute performance,” or Dhrystone Million Instructions Per Second, a typical synthetic benchmark. This is admittedly an old CPU integer benchmark from the 1980s, while NPUs tend to be rated in TOPS, but that figure was not given. For reference, the Topaz NPU add-in card Phison showed at Computex carried eight NPUs rated at up to 40 TOPS each. The DMIPS figure therefore likely refers to, instead, the CPU cores that work alongside it, which we would expect to be based on an Arm Cortex design, adequate for running an SSD of this class but no measure of its AI capability.

The memory and its bandwidth are more important for the expected workload, anyway, and while the company doesn’t state the type, LPDDR5X is a popular option and may be used with the Topaz NPU, according to one secondary report. If the drive is a PCIe 6.0 part comparable to Phison’s X3 controller, it could hit up to 28 GB/s to the host, which would put the memory above 4x that speed. For reference, 130 GB/s is a bit less than half of that of the DGX Spark’s hardware, designed for AI development.

Phison says developers can build on the drive “without specialized programming tools,” which, in this case, is a good thing. The drive does, of course, support multiple general-purpose inference servers and runtimes, but nothing was disclosed about how a host reaches the drive’s compute. The drives “scan and process data in parallel,” which indicates scalability, and return “relevant results” which Phison claims could mean 10 to 100 times less data transferred for applicable workloads. This may free up the host CPU for other tasks.

Phison’s idea to put compute on storage isn’t new. Samsung tried this with its SmartSSD — where an FPGA sat on the drive to process data without moving it — which was shown in 2018. Later, in 2022, the design was refined with AMD’s Xilinx Versal FPGA in its second generation, with Samsung claiming more than 50% lower processing time, up to 70% lower energy consumption, and up to 97% lower server CPU usage for scan-heavy database queries, compared to conventional SSDs. The drive has reportedly since disappeared from Samsung’s lineup, though the first-generation model was still sold under AMD’s Xilinx name as of last year.

ScaleFlux took a narrower route, with compression handled inside the SSD controller. The server doesn’t see this, so the drive looks like any other NVMe SSD, and no application has to change its behavior. The CEO claims it is the only company with computational storage in production at hyperscalers. This suggests that not making customers write new code for the drive is the secret to success, as SmartSSD needed that for its FPGA and for each workload. Phison’s selling point here is flexibility for the client to use code written for common AI frameworks.

Company CTO Sebastien Jean, writing in All About Circuits in 2025, argued that generic computational storage had stalled because “each use case is highly unique and it’s simply not scalable.” He called the idea of running the Linux operating system on a drive with bigger processors a “pie-in-the-sky approach.”

Accelerators have long been widely used in data centers, and Jean’s proposal was to put targeted ones inside the SSD, each running a set operation over a range of logical block addresses (LBAs). By his reckoning, a chassis with 90 PCIe 6.0 SSDs could process data at 2.5 TB/s without affecting the CPU. The design revealed at SC26, though, appears closer to the general-purpose model, possibly due to the increasing relevance of AI and the demand for both memory and compute.

Jean also spoke at the Emerging Storage Technology panel at the conference to discuss future developments, including PCIe 7.0 drives that would operate at “50, 56 gigabytes per second,” which he called “faster than a DDR channel.” With 32 drives at this speed, a single chassis could reach “1.8 terabytes per second.” He described this specifically as computational storage for HPC.

Turning to the Mixed Media solution, that’s the “high-endurance pSLC and high-capacity QLC flash” SSD; it’s a 16-channel part, so not comparable to anything you see in retail. The pSLC portion of the drive has the 4-bit QLC flash act in a 1-bit SLC mode, which trades space for performance and endurance. Phison says the design builds on standard NVMe interfaces and fits into existing infrastructure “without requiring specialized host software.”

Jean elaborated at the same panel that the “endurance group on the drive, which is SLC,” is managed separately from the XLC — 3-bit TLC or 4-bit QLC — and each region gets a namespace, so it appears as multiple drives to the OS. Then, high-write data can be sent specifically to the SLC group, while data in the QLC is likely to be more read-heavy. This follows a general trend of enterprise shifting toward QLC for capacity, but having some of it in SLC mode can help reduce the need for specialized caching.

Right now, high-capacity SSDs usually have separate SSDs to fulfill this role, but that takes up more drive bays. The tradeoff with Phison’s approach is that a single drive failure takes out both capacity and a share of the cache. That said, Phison’s approach is more robust from a cache perspective, as “losing one drive out of 24 would result in a 4% cache loss instead of a 25% loss,” the company says, with the 25% presumably assuming four dedicated cache drives.

Dedicated cache drives need tiering handled by the host or array software, while with Mixed Media each drive holds both tiers and the host simply picks the namespace it wants to write to.

The company is not the first to have this idea, either, as Kioxia presented its Mixed Mode idea during FMS 2025, where each drive has a ratio of pSLC to QLC, set once per drive when it’s first used. Kioxia called 1:4 a “good beginning ratio,” though it said customers typically want pSLC at just 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity. That implementation also had parity protection on the pSLC space across multiple drives. Kioxia similarly marketed the benefit of needing fewer slots and field-replaceable units (FRUs), and its slides even used the term “Mixed Media” in two chart headings.

DapuStor went further with its dual-mode J5060, a QLC SSD with a pSLC region that was in customer validation as of August; it compared the design against “four dedicated SLC drives” in a 24-drive system, the kind of setup that would produce Phison’s 25% figure. Solidigm’s Cloud Storage Acceleration Layer works differently, with a host-side flash translation layer, where it can manage small writes on SLC or TLC drives before writing to QLC in favorable large sequential chunks.

There’s also the Enmotus FuzeDrive P200, previewed at CES 2020 as the MiDrive, which used a Phison controller and seems to be the closest to an ancestor of the current Mixed Media. That consumer drive, which we reviewed in 2021, had SLC and QLC portions that acted distinctly, but they were managed by Enmotus software.

In every drive, behind one 16-channel controller

Separate NVMe endurance groups assigned to namespaces; no specialized host software, Phison says

In every drive, with parity protection on the pSLC space across drives

1:4 as a “good beginning ratio”; customers typically want pSLC at 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity

Ratio set once, creating a pSLC namespace and endurance group

In every drive, as a pSLC region beside the QLC

400GB, 800GB or 1.2TB of pSLC on a 30.72TB drive

Separate SLC and QLC block devices on one drive

In the drive, as a fixed SLC portion of a consumer QLC SSD

Reference system: three 800GB SLC drives and seven 61.44TB QLC drives

Flash translation layer running on the host

It’s also inevitable that these two approaches will be compared to Phison’s other upcoming part, the X3 enterprise SSD controller, a PCIe 6.0 design promising up to 28 GB/s of sequential throughput and 6.8 million IOPS. Those figures are unchanged since Computex in June.

The X3’s IOPS sounds like a lot, but we’ve heard considerable banter about AI needing way more than that, as Nvidia’s roadmap target for PCIe 7.0 is at 100 million IOPS, though that target counts 512-byte reads, a smaller unit than the 4KB that SSD ratings usually use, so the two figures aren’t directly comparable. Jean said at the panel that Phison is “very optimistic” it can achieve that. He added that the eightfold jump in IOPS that comes with moving from 4KB to 512-byte accesses would, on existing NAND, mean “eight times as much power,” or “roughly in the 140 to 180 watt range,” which is beyond reasonable, so perhaps a different solution will be required.

For the X3, Phison told us in June to expect customer samples by the end of the year, with volume coming in the middle of next year, though the SC26 release gives no dates. Notably, Micron and Samsung are already offering PCIe 6.0 SSDs, and the Bravera SC6 controller is expected from Marvell. Phison also says its PS7161, which its site lists as a customer sample, is “the first PCIe 6.0 redriver on the PCI-SIG Integrators List.”

Of the two designs, Mixed Media looks like the nearer product. It builds on standard NVMe features, and DapuStor’s similar dual-mode drive reached customer testing this summer, while the one processor figure given for the computational-storage drive says little about what it can do for AI. What remains to be seen for Mixed Media is its pSLC-to-QLC ratio and how configurable it is. CES and Computex are the notable dates on the 2027 calendar to find out more about both, as well as the X3.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10406. You can now buy a Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist

Valve’s 512GB Steam Machine is available to buy now, but the 2TB model is out of stock.

Valve’s 512GB Steam Machine is available to buy now, but the 2TB model is out of stock.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Valve announced some updates about Steam Machine availability today, including good news if you want to buy one right now and potential bad news about future supply due to RAMaggedon.

The good news: You can now purchase a 512GB Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist. Initially, interested buyers had to get on Valve’s waitlist if they wanted to purchase one, but the console-like PC is now available in an “add-to-cart” status, according to the company. As I write this, the 512GB versions with and without a bundled controller are showing estimated delivery dates of three to five business days on Valve’s website. If Valve sells through its current inventory, the company says it will indicate that the product is out of stock instead of making another waitlist.

The bad news: If you’re on the waitlist for a 2TB model, you might not get a chance to buy one at all, as Valve says there are more people on the waitlist than there are units available. The company is closing the waitlists for its 2TB models.

Valve says that while it’s still building more Steam machines, “memory and storage costs continue to increase at unprecedented levels” and that it doesn’t know the number it can make or “at what price we’ll be able to offer them.” The 2TB Steam Machine is “especially constrained.” The company anticipates “having more information” to share about additional supply in January.

After delaying the Steam Machine’s expected early 2026 launch due to the memory and storage crisis, Valve launched the product in June with a price starting at $1,049 for the 512GB model without a bundled controller. A 2TB model without a controller costs $1,349.

#10403. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10401. Let’s talk about GTA VI

The game is finally here, and there’s plenty to discuss.

The game is finally here, and there’s plenty to discuss.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s hard to believe, but Grand Theft Auto VI is now a real thing that we all can play. Thirteen years after GTA V, and eight years since Red Dead Redemption 2, Rockstar is back with its latest sprawling, extravagant open world. There’s a lot to experience — and a lot to talk about.

Perhaps the most impressive thing about GTA VI is how flexible it is, letting you mold the experience to your own play style. You can still be a maniacal criminal, but you can also focus on the new love story that includes an entire button dedicated to holding hands. Or you can spend the hours fishing or driving around listening to the radio. Maybe you just want to doomscroll through a fake version of Instagram. As GTA VI producer Rob Nelson told me just ahead of launch, “It’s a risk. It’s a real change for Grand Theft Auto. You can still play it the old way, you can reject all of this stuff. But for people who do engage, it’s meaningful — you’re earning it, you’re working at it, just like in a normal relationship.”

Now that the game is out and people are finding their own way through Leonida, it’s a great time to start talking about it. I’ve played through the entire main story — you can read my review right here — but I’m nowhere close to experiencing everything in the game. I’ll also be joined by senior reporter Jay Peters, who is still early in the game and can offer a different perspective. There’s a good chance some other Verge staff will stop by as well, since we’re basically all playing it.

So drop all of your questions, theories, and stray thoughts about GTA VI here. I’ll be checking in throughout the day to join the conversation in the comments.

#10397. VAST says petabyte SSDs will outgrow today’s parallel file system RAID

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Built from 15 TB drives, VAST’s 900 PB system would draw about 5.6 MW instead of 400 kW.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

VAST Data says petabyte SSDs will outgrow RAID in today’s parallel file systems, according to a simulation of its own 900 PB system that puts the odds of data loss at 1 in 11 over 10 years. At Supercomputing 2026 (SC26), the company explains how.

Bigger drives take longer to rebuild after a failure, leaving more time for further failures to lose data, but operators will still want those large drives because many sites are power-constrained and storage power scales with bandwidth rather than capacity. VAST’s recommended fix: system-wide erasure coding with at least three parity strips.

The company’s principal technical strategist, Glenn Lockwood, walked through the simulation, described as a “discrete event simulation” using real failure data, in an Exhibitor Forum talk during SC26’s HPC System Hardware and Architecture session. With petabyte-scale SSDs swapped in, enclosure-local declustered RAID — the approach used by DDN EXAScaler and HPE Cray ClusterStor, per Lockwood — has those exceptionally high odds of data loss. With global declustering and extra parity, operators can get this down to 1 in 54 million, a significant improvement, but VAST’s own scheme gets to about 1 in 62 billion, three orders of magnitude better.

Lockwood’s case for petabyte SSDs rests on how storage power scales: at about 28 W per SSD, regardless of capacity and including its share of the surrounding infrastructure, plus 20 W per GB/s of bandwidth. Getting 900 PB of flash from 15 TB SSDs would take more than 63K drives, each adding its own bandwidth, for 190 TB/s at 5.6 MW, or “the equivalent of 3,100 or so of Blackwell Ultra GPUs.” Lockwood says, though, that “real AI applications in our experience have never approached needing anything close to that level of performance” from the storage.

With 122 TB drives, at almost an 8K count and much lower 8 TB/s of aggregate bandwidth, the power draw is less than a tenth of that, at about 400 kW. Within that budget, 15 TB SSDs top out at about 250 PB, even with no power left for performance. That power could be better spent on compute and, anyway, 245 TB-class drives are already arriving, with much larger drives expected.

Everything is a series of trade-offs, though, and larger drives come with their own caveats. Lockwood acknowledges the usual reaction to a 245 TB drive: that it’s “a big blast radius,” since a single failure leaves much more to rebuild. And it does take longer: at the same per-drive speed, a 122 TB drive takes twice as long to rebuild as a 60 TB drive, leaving a longer window in which a second failure in a traditional RAID 5 set loses data. Per-drive capacity is potentially outpacing bandwidth increases and, at least within a generation, “the performance of each individual drive does not change,” so this is something that must be accepted or overcome.

The simulated system involves 7,920 SSDs at 122 TB each, using 180 enclosures with 44 drives each. With a RAID 5 single parity solution, there is a 100% chance of data loss over 10 years, but “no one uses single parity anymore.” Double parity still has 1 in 150 odds of data loss over a decade and is considered “state of the art for many open source file systems,” and he explicitly names Lustre and BeeGFS within this context.

For proprietary parallel file system implementations, declustered RAID within an enclosure is the state of the art, instead. Declustered RAID spreads each drive group’s data, parity, and spare space over more drives than usual, allowing for faster rebuilds. Keeping it within an enclosure means that the spread still stays within a single box of drives. Swapping petabyte SSDs into that enclosure-level declustering is what gets to the headline 1 in 11 odds of data loss.

As for the mentioned rivals, DDN’s DCR, in one published configuration, uses RAID 6 in an 8+2 array for a 24-NVMe SSD pool. That means eight data strips plus two parity strips per stripe, allowing any two drives in the pool to fail without losing data, and each stripe is spread over 10 of the 24 drives. DDN has conceded in the past that larger systems may need more than DCR. HPE’s declustered RAID solution is known as GridRAID, with declustered parity per enclosure half, meaning two 12-drive pools per 24-drive enclosure.

Perlmutter, the supercomputer at Berkeley Lab’s National Energy Research Scientific Computing Center, where Lockwood once worked, used GridRAID with 8+2+1 RAID 6 per 12-drive object storage target, Lustre’s name for a storage unit. The +1 is one spare drive’s worth of space, spread across all 12, so a rebuild writes to every surviving drive rather than to a single replacement. Either way, protection stops at two failures in a pool of one or two dozen drives, and only those drives can assist with a rebuild. This is the scenario in VAST’s simulation with the catastrophic failure rate.

Declustered RAID across the whole system, plus extra parity

Several solutions exist, with limits. Adding a parity strip for triple parity reduces the odds of data loss. Global declustering, on its own, can also reduce the odds a little bit, with faster rebuild times. However, stripes are spread over every drive in the cluster, so two random failures are more likely to hit the same stripe, meaning a larger fault domain.

If both approaches are used, which is the minimum bar for him, you get down to getting struck by “lightning on your birthday” odds. VAST already publishes quadruple parity, which is beyond this bar. Using VAST’s own “special magic,” undisclosed but beyond the minimum bar, drops the odds even further, specifically for the petabyte SSDs, which is the real target of the talk.

Then we get to the metadata, the data about data, which in this case is the index of where every piece of every file dwells. Every write necessarily updates it synchronously, so VAST keeps it on separate, fast SSDs as three full copies for replication, not parity. This means it can survive two failures, which is less than the data’s four parity strips, and the index becomes the weakest link. With 122 TB drives, he puts the data drives’ odds at 1 in 78 trillion over 10 years, against 1 in 58 million for metadata. To Lockwood, this illustrates that the surrounding infrastructure is the real risk, given the failure gap between the index and regular data.

Rebuilding in the index area starts about 12 seconds after a failure, on average, versus about two minutes for data drives, Lockwood says. VAST’s procedure more generally, for when a drive drops out, is to power-cycle it and hope, so there is always some delay before a rebuild is forced. This slight delay barely matters for data drives with four parity strips but is more impactful for the three-copy metadata index drives. For example, waiting an hour for data drives “wouldn’t make a big difference” while just 20 minutes or more on a metadata drive has “extremely high” risk.

Earlier this year, in May, Micron began shipping the 245 TB 6600 ION, which pulls up to 30 W at maximum load. Kioxia has the LC9, used in a Dell 2U server with 40 drives for a total of 9.8 PB. SanDisk also has its 256 TB UltraQLC, which was due in the first half of this year, and Solidigm is targeting about 245 TB later this year.

Meanwhile, multiple roadmaps reach petabyte-class drives, including SanDisk’s UltraQLC, a target by SK hynix for QLC SSDs, and Samsung at some time in the future. This strengthens VAST’s case and, while at least the power math adds up, data still needs to be compared openly and fairly to make a firm conclusion about the best pathway forward for what is clearly an unavoidable problem.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.
