#10283. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

#10278. vLLM V0 to V1: Correctness Before Corrections in RL

#10277. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

#10276. Hands-on: Sony’s Walkman-revival USB-C headphones are back in stock and worth a look

Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly.

The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.

While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.

The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.

The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box.

As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.

For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me.

Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

#10275. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

#10273. Clicks Communicator hits a supply chain snag, and it isn’t because of RAM

Clicks is still on track to release the phone with a physical keyboard within the next few months, but a Communicator launch before the end of this year isn’t looking very likely as shipping gets pushed back.

The Communicator is a fresh take on what an Android phone can be. It was first introduced at CES earlier this year, with the main selling angle being the physically integrated keyboard. Clicks wants to target those who are looking for a second device, and the Communicator fits the bill.

Last we heard, the Clicks Communicator was on track to launch and ship in December, following a cheery update detailing an upgrade to 12GB of RAM and other changes. However, Clicks posted a new update today stating that the timeline has shifted out a month because of supply chain issues caused by something other than RAM.

Unfortunately, we’ve encountered a delay with securing a critical supply chain component needed to fabricate our printed circuit board ahead of manufacturing and final assembly. Assembly will now begin in early January, with deliveries expected to start in late January.

It notes that the “critical supply chain component” is the essential fabric used for the company’s printed circuit boards. The blog post dives into some neat details about the component holding things up. The PCBs Clicks is using are made up of more material layers than some competitor boards. But the fabric used to layer those boards is in short supply at this sort of demand, with four manufacturers reportedly unable to meet the company’s required volume.

The post also spells out difficulties caused by troubles in the Middle East, and rising memory costs straining the chain of supply further, even if memory – surprisingly – isn’t the cause of this small delay. Those compounded issues have pushed production back, and users are now told to expect their Clicks Communicator pre-orders to be shipped in late January, at the earliest. Many pre-orders will likely be shipped after that because of further time constraints before Chinese New Year.

In an act of good faith, Clicks says it’s including a set of 3.5mm wired headphones in each box for orders placed prior to today.

A push from December to January isn’t massive, and Clicks still seems to be making solid time on developing a form factor we don’t often see. You can still pre-order the Clicks Communicator starting at $649.

#10267. How Consumer AI Companions Handle Context Windows, Persistent Memory, and Voice Latency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI companions are the most demanding consumer use of large language models right now. A coding assistant can forget your last message and still be useful. A companion that forgets your name, your running story, or the plan you made last Tuesday has failed at its one job. On top of that, users now expect voice calls that feel like a phone conversation, not a walkie-talkie exchange with a five-second pause.

Three engineering problems decide whether a companion app works: how much of the conversation fits in the model's context window, what the app remembers once the window fills up, and how fast the voice pipeline answers. Here is how consumer companions handle each one, with real tier and pricing examples, and what developers can take from their designs for their own products.

Most LLM products are judged on single answers. Companions are judged on continuity. That creates pressure in three places at once:

Every consumer companion is a different set of trade-offs across those three constraints, and the trade-offs are increasingly how the apps are priced.

The context window is the total number of tokens the model can see when it writes the next reply. In a companion app, that budget is spent before the user says anything:

When the chat log outgrows what is left, the oldest messages fall out. The user experiences this as the companion "forgetting" mid-conversation. This is why context size is now a headline spec in the category, and a paid feature. SpicyChat AI, for example, gives free accounts a 4K context window, then scales memory to 8K on its $19.95/mo tier and 32K on its $29.95/mo tier. Janitor AI runs about 9K of context on its own JanitorLLM. Independent uncensored AI chat benchmarks record these context sizes alongside refusal behavior and pricing, because context length predicts how long a roleplay stays coherent better than any marketing claim.

Some apps sell memory in messages rather than tokens. Fantasy GF's pricing page lists a 60-message base "smart memory" and 5x that, 300 messages, on its Ultra plan. Others sell it as a memory multiplier on higher subscription tiers. The pattern is consistent across the category: memory depth is the upgrade lever, not the model itself.

No consumer app keeps an entire relationship in the prompt. Persistent memory is a separate system that writes to storage and reads back into the context window on each turn. In practice it has three layers:

The differences users notice are usually retention policies, not algorithms. Crushon AI's own subscription docs say free-plan chat history is deleted after 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans keep it for the membership period. Paid tiers across the category commonly promise that memories never expire. There is also a security angle worth stating plainly: companion chat logs are among the most sensitive data a consumer product can hold. A 2024 breach at Muah.ai, first reported by 404 Media, exposed roughly 1.9 million users' email addresses together with their private prompts. Any app that builds persistent memory is also building a liability, and developers copying the pattern should treat the memory store as the most protected part of the system.

A voice call in a companion app is three models chained together: speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech. Run them strictly in sequence and a single reply can take several seconds, which reads as dead air. The apps that feel fast attack each stage the same way:

With every stage streaming, time-to-first-audio can land close to a second on a good connection, which is roughly the threshold where a conversation stops feeling laggy. Voice is also expensive to run - three model calls instead of one, plus audio bandwidth - which is why it is metered harder than text. Fantasy GF charges 8 tokens per minute for voice calls (4 on Ultra), which turns the 100 monthly tokens on its entry plan into about 12 minutes of call time. SpicyChat, for its part, only adds text-to-speech at its top tier. Latency engineering and unit economics are the same problem here: faster pipelines cost less per call, and cheaper calls are what make voice viable below the top plan.

Tier details below come from each app's own pricing or documentation pages, checked November 16, 2026, and cross-checked against il.ly's AI companion testing guide, which hands-tests 15 companion apps across memory, media, and voice.

Two takeaways from the table. First, nobody sells a bigger base model - they sell a bigger window into the same one. Second, the free tiers are deliberately memory-poor: limited context and short retention are the upgrade prompt.

Even if you never build a companion, these apps are a useful reference architecture for any stateful LLM product:

Consumer AI companions have turned three hard infrastructure problems - bounded context, durable memory, and real-time voice - into their pricing pages. Context size, memory depth, and voice access are the tiers. If you are building anything stateful on an LLM, this category is worth watching closely: it is solving continuity and latency problems at consumer scale, in public, one subscription tier at a time.

It is the total amount of text, measured in tokens, that the model can consider when writing its next reply. The character definition, stored memories, recent chat, and the new message all share that budget. When the conversation exceeds it, the oldest content drops out and the companion appears to forget.

Yes, through a memory system separate from the context window. Apps summarize older chats, extract stable facts about you into a profile, and retrieve relevant past moments into the prompt. Retention varies: some free tiers delete chat history after as little as 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans typically keep memory for the membership period.

A voice turn runs three models - speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech - instead of one, plus audio transfer. Providers pass that cost on through per-minute token charges or by locking voice behind higher tiers.

For casual chat, 4K to 9K tokens works. For long-running roleplay or a companion expected to hold plot threads for weeks, 32K and up, or a well-built memory layer on top of a smaller window, makes the visible difference.

#10259. 5 Things Most Developers Get Wrong When Turning Images into HTML

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Turning a design image into a working webpage can look simple.

You have a picture (a mockup, a screenshot, a Figma export), and your job is to turn it into working HTML in a browser.

But here is the catch. A design image is static, while a webpage is dynamic and responsive across different screen sizes and devices. A webpage needs to load quickly, adapt perfectly to different screen sizes, and allow touch interactions on mobile devices.

Most mistakes in this process happen when the conversion is treated as copying a picture instead of rebuilding it.

This section walks you through the mistakes developers often make, and more importantly, how to fix each of them.

Whether you are slicing a client mockup or converting a screenshot into code, avoiding these mistakes can save you from hours of rework. Let’s get into the details.

A screenshot reflects how a page appears at a particular display. But it doesn’t show how the layout will behave when the content changes or the screen gets smaller.

Developers sometimes try to copy the exact positions and dimensions of every element. To match the screenshot, they may use fixed widths, large margins, and absolute positioning.

This can work at one screen size, but the layout may break on smaller screens or when a heading wraps onto an extra line.

The better approach is identifying the layout rules behind the design. CSS Grid can arrange the cards in columns. Flexbox can help align elements within each card. And media queries can adjust the layout for smaller screens.

Pixel-level comparison can be useful in some cases, but it should not come at the cost of a flexible layout.

When developers recreate a design, it’s easier for them to focus on shapes and spacing rather than the purpose of each element. So, they may use generic elements for headings, buttons, navigation areas, and content sections instead of choosing elements that reflect each element's purpose.

This may produce the right appearance, but it often makes the code harder to maintain and understand. And when developers fail to use the right HTML elements, it can also create accessibility problems later.

So don’t put everything inside several unnamed containers. Instead, use a clear structure with appropriate headings, meaningful alt text for informative images, and real buttons for actions.

Semantic elements (e.g., header, nav, main, section, and footer) can also help organize a large page when they match the content’s purpose.

A design image may have headings, descriptions, prices, labels, or button text.

Reusing the entire image to reproduce the original design feels quick. But it can create problems when that text has to work as part of a responsive web page.

For example, there’s a promotional banner that contains a heading, a short description, and a CTA button. If you use the entire banner as one image, you can’t easily change the heading, translate the text, or make the button work as a real element.

So, don’t treat the image like that. Instead, use the original image where appropriate and recreate the text and button in HTML. CSS can control their position, size, color, and spacing.

Make sure you distinguish between visual elements that should remain images and text that should be recreated as HTML.

Not every part of turning images into HTML needs to be done manually.

Developers sometimes spend too much time on recreating basic elements when there are free online conversion tools available. These tools can help them get an initial version much faster.

An online conversion tool may be helpful when you need to extract text or generate a rough starting layout from a design image.

For example, you can upload a design image into an online image to html converter to get a starting point (rather than starting from a blank file). Review the tool’s output. Identify the parts you can reuse (as they are) and tweak the parts that need adjustments.

Keep in mind that the first result generated by the tool isn’t the final code. You may need to reorganize the HTML, refine the CSS, and adjust the layout to make it responsive.

Use automation where it saves time and your own judgement where it matters.

A screenshot shows just one view of a page. It doesn’t show how the layout may change on a phone or how people using a keyboard/screen reader can interact with it.

So, developers who focus only on the desktop design may end up with:

content that becomes too small on mobile devices

There’s also a chance of accessibility problems when images lack suitable alt text/interactive elements.

Let’s say a page has three cards in a row. On the desktop, this arrangement may work well. But on a phone, you may need to stack the cards vertically so their content remains readable.

That’s why accessibility should be considered during the same process. Use meaningful headings and provide appropriate alt text for images. Also, maintain sufficient text contrast and make sure interactive elements can be operated with a keyboard.

Lastly, don’t forget to test the page on both desktop and mobile screen sizes before finalizing it.

HTML and CSS handle the structure and appearance of a webpage, but JavaScript makes it possible to add interactive features.

For example, a design image may contain a navigation menu with a button. When recreating this design, developers should make sure the button performs an actual action rather than simply looking like a clickable element.

You can use JavaScript's event listeners to handle these interactions.

Now, add JavaScript to make the menu functional:

In this example, clicking the button opens or closes the navigation menu. The aria-expanded attribute also communicates the menu's current state to assistive technologies.

This is a simple example of why converting a design image into HTML involves more than matching its appearance. Developers also need to recreate the behavior of interactive elements using appropriate HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

The process of turning an image into HTML feels easy when you treat it the right way.

Start by identifying the main sections, text, images, and interactive elements. Then create meaningful HTML and build the layout with CSS. Make the necessary adjustments to ensure it works across different screen sizes.

Use a conversion tool when it can really save time. But don’t forget to review the generated output of the tool thoroughly before using it.

A screenshot is a visual reference, not a complete set of development instructions. And the best result for you isn’t simply a page that looks like the image.

It is a page that also works well, adapts well, and stays easy to maintain.

#10258. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

#10257. Best Buy brings back 4K Blu-ray discs with an expanding collection, in-store availability [U]

It’s been nearly three years since Best Buy decided to ditch selling physical copies of movies, stripping its inventory from both its brick-and-mortar stores and its online catalog. But with the growing rise of 4K Blu-ray collections as a vinyl-esque collector’s item, the electronics giant has slowly begun dipping its toes back into the physical media game.

As spotted by TheHDRoom on Twitter and on the 4K Blu-ray subreddit, Best Buy is now selling 14 different 4K Blu-rays on its website, marking another step in the brand’s slow return to selling physical media. These films include:

Technically, Blu-ray returned to Best Buy’s online storefront in August of 2025, when it opened a third-party marketplace similar to Amazon or Walmart. These titles, however, are shipped and sold by Best Buy. Everything’s still online-only, unfortunately, save for The Odyssey, which launched in-store shelf displays alongside yesterday’s release date. It shouldn’t come as too much of a surprise that The Odyssey is getting some special treatment; in addition to its status as a box office juggernaut, Nolan’s previous movie, Oppenheimer, initially sold out of physical copies in its first week of release in 2023.

Outside of these 14 titles, additional Blu-rays continue to be sold through Best Buy’s third-party marketplace.

Update, 11/20: Best Buy confirmed to The HD Room on Twitter that The Odyssey is just the first 4K Blu-ray to return to physical locations, as it plans to continue adding “some” Blu-rays back to its stores.

The Odyssey may/will be joined by other 4K UHD titles at Best Buy in the future: "The Odyssey inspired a homecoming of our own – some physical media is returning to Best Buy. Sometimes what’s old does become new (again). And we're still feeling nostalgic, so stay tuned." pic.twitter.com/wz9xhcdo4G

#10254. Nintendo Switch 2-ready smart glasses from Viture are at their lowest price ever (up to $160 off)

Viture’s smart display glasses offer official Switch 2 compatibility with the Mobile Dock Mini, and these Black Friday deals even come with free bonus controllers and other accessories.

One of the most annoying things about playing Ocarina of Time right now is that everyone in my home is at a different point in their save file. This makes it tough to be in the same room with others and not spoil something for them, but plugging in a pair of Viture glasses makes this much easier since no one else can see the screen inside the glasses I’m wearing.

You can get your own pair of Viture glasses with the Switch 2 Mobile Dock Mini for as low as $375. This pack comes with Viture Pro 2 glasses, which sport a pair of 120Hz micro-OLED displays with 1,600-nit peak brightness and built-in myopia adjustment diopters. They also weigh just 63g, making them comfortable for long gaming sessions.

The Mobile Dock Mini (on sale for $99 on its own) in the pack comes with a magnetic mount that attaches to the back of the Switch 2. This Dock lets you plug your Viture glasses in and also charges the Switch 2, thanks to the built-in 10,000mAh battery. That delivers more than double the Switch 2’s battery life, so you can clear several temples in Ocarina before even thinking of stopping to charge.

Most of Viture’s glasses are on sale and have extra free gifts, which you can see in the image above. This includes the company’s high-end Viture Beast model, which is on sale for $489 ($60 off). That’s the lowest price ever for the glasses.

Viture Beast upgrades the experience with a wider field of view (58 degrees), native 3DoF spatial tracking, and 9-level electrochromic lens dimming. Native spatial tracking means that you can pin the virtual monitor in space and move around as if it were a TV mounted on a wall. Beast doesn’t have diopters, so you’ll need to order prescription lenses if you need myopia adjustment.

The biggest discount of all is on Viture Luma Pro, which is down to $339 ($160 off). Luma Pro is Viture’s “Goldilocks” pair with excellent image quality and RGB lighting along the frames for some extra style, and even includes myopia adjustment diopters. Pair any of these Viture glasses with the Mobile Dock Mini for Switch 2 compatibility, making them the only pair of smart glasses with official Switch 2 support.

Lastly is Viture Luma Ultra, down to $449 ($150 off). Luma Ultra sports the best image quality of all Viture glasses and includes myopia-adjusting diopters. These are the only pair that support 6DoF tracking when using the Viture Spacewalker app thanks to the cameras on the front, but there’s no native 3DoF support like Beast has, so this functionality is limited to content you view through the app.

#10250. Retail Merchandising Principles for Web Designers and Developers: Display Design, Ranking Rules and Measurement

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every e-commerce grid, product page and checkout screen is a shelf. Merchandisers have spent decades working out what to put at eye level, how many choices to show and how to tell whether a display works, and web teams are now rebuilding the same logic in code: sort orders, pinned products, badges, filters and A/B tests. Knowing where those ideas came from helps you build them with fewer mistakes.

This article maps physical retail merchandising onto digital product design. It covers visual hierarchy and choice load in listing UIs, a small rule-based ranking function you can test, accessible price and badge design with a contrast checker, and a significance test for layout experiments. It also covers why good plans fail at the handoff between design, engineering and operations. The numbers in the examples are illustrative, so use your own data. The code was run with Node.js 22.

Merchandising shapes how products are displayed, promoted and made accessible across sales channels. A well-planned strategy considers visual hierarchy, packaging, promotional materials, inventory timing and consistency between locations. For example, a skincare product may struggle to convert if testers are missing, shelf information is unclear or displays arrive after a campaign begins. If you build for the web, nearly every one of these problems has a direct counterpart:

Customers scan before they read. Nielsen Norman Group defines visual hierarchy as the arrangement of elements so the eye moves through them in order of importance, and names color and contrast, scale and grouping as the main tools. On a shelf, a larger facing or a contrasting background makes the hero product visible first. In a product grid, the equivalents are a larger card for a featured item, a clear boundary around a bundle, and a restrained use of badges so that only one or two items carry emphasis.

A common mistake is giving every card a badge, a color and a call to action. When everything is emphasized, nothing is, and the page reads like a crowded table in a store. Treat emphasis as a limited resource and have the design system say how many emphasized cards one screen may show.

A premium item placed beside entry-level alternatives may lose perceived value, and a crowded display can make fresh products look less appealing. Hick’s Law states that the time to make a decision increases with the number and complexity of choices, and its practical advice is to reduce options when speed matters and to highlight recommended ones. In a listing UI that suggests a curated first screen with a clear sort label, a few high-confidence recommendations and filters that handle the long tail, rather than an unbroken wall of products. Hick’s Law is a guide, not a rule, so treat it as a hypothesis to test on your own audience.

Merchandisers often ask for things like “pin the new range to the top, boost this collection, never show more than two items from one brand in a row, and push sold-out items down”. Implemented as scattered CSS order values and one-off queries, these requests become hard to audit. A small, pure function keeps the rules explicit and testable.

Because the function has no side effects, a short test can check every rule. This one confirms that pinned items lead, a pinned item that is out of stock is skipped, the brand cap pushes a third item from one brand behind other brands, sold-out items go last and a boost can lift a weaker product:

The design decision here is that stock demotion and the brand cap are defaults, while pins and boosts are inputs a merchandiser can change without a deploy, for example from a config file or admin screen. A real system would also log which rule moved each item, so that someone can answer why a product appears where it does.

Price cards and shelf labels are text, and text needs contrast. The WCAG contrast requirement asks for at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text. Product cards are a place where this slips, because grey secondary prices and pastel sale badges look refined in a mockup. The following script computes the WCAG contrast ratio, so you can run it over your design tokens in CI.

It prints 4.54 for mid grey #767676 on white, which just passes for normal text, 7.00 for #595959, which would also meet the stricter enhanced level, and 3.96 for the pink #d94f70, which fails for small badge text but would pass for large text. A sale badge that fails the check should be fixed in the token, not on one page.

A useful display respects the realities of the sales environment: floor space, customer movement, staff workload, replenishment access and how much information shoppers can absorb at a glance. The digital version is the gap between the design file and what ships. Test the setup with the people who will use it, not only with the team that created it, because their feedback reveals practical problems before a wide rollout.

A polished plan can fail in practice when materials arrive late, displays are assembled incorrectly or teams miss replenishment. On the web, the matching failures are a promotion that goes live before its product data is synced, a badge that stays on after a sale ends, and a hero banner that links to a discontinued item. Execution needs coordination across marketing, suppliers, logistics and engineering, with clear instructions, realistic schedules, display standards and checks. Some companies outsource this kind of field merchandising. One provider’s services page, for example, lists planogram and retail resets, end-cap displays, signage placement, on-shelf availability, in-store audits and visit reporting. Whether or not you hire help, that list is a useful checklist of what execution involves, and most items have a digital twin you can automate: availability checks, expiry dates on promotions and audit reports. Treat any performance figures on vendor pages as the vendor’s own claims and ask for evidence before relying on them.

Timing matters most for seasonal promotions and limited windows. A campaign that goes live after peak demand has passed cannot deliver the same value, so give promotional content a start and end date in the data model and have a scheduled job remove it, rather than relying on someone to remember.

Sales alone cannot tell you whether visibility, placement, stockouts, confusing information or poor setup caused a result. For a web listing, the answer is a controlled experiment. The script below compares conversion for a control grid and a variant with a two-proportion z-test, a standard way to check whether a difference is larger than chance would explain.

With these illustrative numbers, conversion rises from 3.00 percent to 3.90 percent, a relative lift of 30.0 percent, and the z value of 2.21 exceeds the 1.96 threshold for roughly 95 percent confidence. That does not prove the curated grid is better in general. It says this difference is unlikely to be noise for this test, and it assumes you fixed the sample size in advance and did not stop the moment the result looked good. Use a proper experimentation platform or a statistician for anything with real revenue at stake.

A strong merchandising operation does not require a large budget. A simple guide for product priorities, layout, pricing, replenishment and approved materials keeps execution consistent, and it is the physical version of a design system: a small set of approved components and rules that make the correct result the easy path. For a product card, that means a documented component with a fixed set of badge types, a defined maximum of emphasized items per screen and tokens that already pass the contrast check.

Consistency also protects trust. Damaged fixtures, inconsistent packaging, missing prices and outdated signs make good products look poorly managed, and the digital equivalents are broken images, stale prices and mismatched labels between the listing and the product page. Aim for consistency, not identical pages: a mobile grid and a kiosk screen can differ, but they should share product priorities, visual identity and core messages.

Temporary and in-store selling spaces demand quick communication. Visitors move fast, attention is limited, and interfaces must be clear from a distance and forgiving of mistakes. If you build kiosk, tablet or point-of-sale software, these constraints shape the interface:

When results disappoint, audit the real experience before commissioning a redesign. Think like a first-time customer: is the offer clear, are premium items distinct and can the team maintain the page easily? Government small-business guidance offers concrete analogues. The Queensland Government’s retail display guidance recommends showing essential information and prices so customers can decide quickly, placing popular or profitable items at eye level, and refreshing window displays every two to four weeks. The Westminster City Council guide similarly links visual merchandising, layout and planned promotions to attracting customers and encouraging them to spend more time and money in a business.

Prioritize visibility, clarity, stock accuracy and consistent execution before investing in a full redesign. Small operational improvements can make a meaningful difference, especially when they remove obstacles that repeatedly interrupt the buying process.

The public guidance cited here was written for general small-business use, and UX principles such as visual hierarchy and Hick’s Law come from interface research, so they are useful analogies rather than guarantees. The code is a teaching sketch: it ignores personalization, pagination, caching, search relevance and many other concerns of a production ranking system. Results vary with category, audience and device, so test changes in your own setting and treat any benchmark as a starting point.

What is merchandising in e-commerce? It is how products are displayed, ordered, promoted and made accessible across a site or app, including layout, labels, badges, sort rules and inventory handling.

How does visual hierarchy apply to a product grid? The same tools apply: contrast, scale and grouping decide what the eye sees first and which products read as related.

What should I measure first? Start with conversion, add-to-cart rate and stock availability for the listing, then add average order value and revenue per visitor once the basics are tracked.

How do I know a layout test is reliable? Fix the sample size in advance, randomize users, run it long enough to cover weekly patterns and check significance, for example with the two-proportion test above.

Do small teams need a big design system? No. A short component guide with a few badge types, spacing rules and accessible color tokens is enough to keep pages consistent.

Merchandising is applied interface design with a long history. Clear hierarchy, a curated first screen, readable prices, explicit ranking rules and controlled experiments carry over directly from the shelf to the screen. For designers and developers, the practical lesson is that a good layout only counts if it ships correctly, stays current and is measured. Audit the basics, put the rules in code, test one change at a time and record what you learn.

#10248. Samsung might copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island soon, but it actually looks good [Video]

A new leak suggests that Samsung is preparing to copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island feature in the not-too-distant future, to an extent that not many other Android brands have even done thus far.

Copying the Dynamic Island was briefly like a rite of passage for some Android brands, with many wasting almost no time in delivering their own version of the feature. But, on many of those devices, it’s a surface-level ripoff at best. The feature is primarily limited to music apps and timers, rarely working with any third-party apps in meaningful ways. Android has a built-in feature similar to Apple’s but not in the same styling.

Samsung is now preparing its own version of the Dynamic Island, and it seems this one has a bit more meat on its bones.

SamMobile on Twitter/X posted a video and two still images of what appears to be a Dynamic Island clone coming to Galaxy devices. It’s mentioned as potentially coming to the Galaxy S27 series (so, One UI 9.5) and being called “Moment Effects.” A portion of the software explicitly mentions integration with Google Sports, the leak claims, while it seems Samsung is using Live Notifications here, which already support a number of third-party apps.

The kicker from a UX perspective is that Samsung’s interpretation allows you to swipe between activities, similar to Apple, but the core premise is similar with the island showing around the selfie camera cutout.

When this feature might surface does remain unclear, but a One UI 9.5 debut would make plenty of sense. That update is in beta now and due to launch on the Galaxy S27 series early next year.

What do you think of Samsung’s alleged Dynamic Island clone? Personally, I’m on board as long as it proves useful, and it seems to be on the right track compared to most other copycats.
